The canonization was celebrated in Rome's Saint Peter's Square on 14 October 2018.<sup id="cite_ref-120" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-120">&#91;120&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Previously, there had been hopes that Romero would be canonized during a possible papal visit to El Salvador on 15 August 2017 – the centennial of the late bishop's birth – or that he could be canonized in <a href="/wiki/Panama" title="Panama">Panama</a> during <a href="/wiki/World_Youth_Day_2019" title="World Youth Day 2019">World Youth Day</a> in 2019.<sup id="cite_ref-121" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-121">&#91;121&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Romero was the first Salvadoran to be raised to the altars; the first martyred archbishop of America, the first to be declared a martyr after the <a href="/wiki/Second_Vatican_Council" title="Second Vatican Council">Second Vatican Council</a>;<sup id="cite_ref-122" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-122">&#91;122&#93;</a></sup> and the first native saint of <a href="/wiki/Central_America" title="Central America">Central America</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-123" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-123">&#91;123&#93;</a></sup> (<a href="/wiki/Peter_of_Saint_Joseph_de_Betancur" title="Peter of Saint Joseph de Betancur">Peter of Saint Joseph de Betancur</a>, who did all his work for which he was canonized in the city of <a href="/wiki/Santiago_de_los_Caballeros" title="Santiago de los Caballeros">Santiago de los Caballeros</a> of <a href="/wiki/Guatemala" title="Guatemala">Guatemala</a>, was from <a href="/wiki/Tenerife" title="Tenerife">Tenerife</a>, <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-124" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-124">&#91;124&#93;</a></sup>) Romero had already been included on the <a href="/wiki/Anglican_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Anglican Church">Anglican Church</a>'s list of official saints<sup id="cite_ref-125" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-125">&#91;125&#93;</a></sup> and on the <a href="/wiki/Lutheran_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran Church">Lutheran Church</a>'s liturgical calendar.<sup id="cite_ref-126" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-126">&#91;126&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Homages_and_cultural_references">Homages and cultural references</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=%C3%93scar_Romero&amp;action=edit&amp;section=24" title="Edit section: Homages and cultural references">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<figure class="mw-default-size" typeof="mw:File/Thumb"><a href="/wiki/File:SalvadorDelMundo.jpg" class="mw-file-description"><img src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/SalvadorDelMundo.jpg/170px-SalvadorDelMundo.jpg" decoding="async" width="170" height="266" class="mw-file-element" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/SalvadorDelMundo.jpg/255px-SalvadorDelMundo.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/13/SalvadorDelMundo.jpg/340px-SalvadorDelMundo.jpg 2x" data-file-width="409" data-file-height="640" /></a><figcaption><a href="/wiki/Monumento_al_Divino_Salvador_del_Mundo" title="Monumento al Divino Salvador del Mundo">Monumento al Divino Salvador del Mundo</a> in Plaza Salvador del Mundo</figcaption></figure>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Institutions">Institutions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=%C3%93scar_Romero&amp;action=edit&amp;section=25" title="Edit section: Institutions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>The Romero Centre in <a href="/wiki/Dublin" title="Dublin">Dublin</a>, Ireland, is today an important centre that "promotes Development Education, Arts, Crafts, and Awareness about El Salvador."<sup id="cite_ref-127" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-127">&#91;127&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Christian_Initiative_Romero" title="Christian Initiative Romero">Christian Initiative Romero</a> is a non-profit organization in Germany working in support of industrial law and human rights in Central American countries.<sup id="cite_ref-Ci-RomeroAboutus_128-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Ci-RomeroAboutus-128">&#91;128&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/Romero_Institute" title="Romero Institute">Romero Institute</a>, a nonprofit law and public policy center in <a href="/wiki/Santa_Cruz,_California" title="Santa Cruz, California">Santa Cruz, California</a>, U.S., headed by <a href="/wiki/Daniel_Sheehan_(attorney)" title="Daniel Sheehan (attorney)">Daniel Sheehan</a>, was named after Archbishop Romero in 1996.<sup id="cite_ref-129" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-129">&#91;129&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In 1989 the <a href="/wiki/Toronto_Catholic_District_School_Board" title="Toronto Catholic District School Board">Toronto Catholic District School Board</a> opened a secondary school in <a href="/wiki/Toronto" title="Toronto">Toronto</a>, Canada, named after Archbishop Óscar Romero called <a href="/wiki/St._Oscar_Romero_Catholic_Secondary_School" title="St. Oscar Romero Catholic Secondary School">St. Oscar Romero Catholic Secondary School</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-About_Us_130-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-About_Us-130">&#91;130&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>St. Oscar Romero Catholic High School<sup id="cite_ref-131" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-131">&#91;131&#93;</a></sup> in Edmonton, Canada, formerly known as Archbishop Oscar Romero, and as Blessed Oscar Romero throughout his canonization.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/St_Oscar_Romero_Catholic_School" title="St Oscar Romero Catholic School">St Oscar Romero Catholic School</a>, a coeducational secondary school in <a href="/wiki/Worthing" title="Worthing">Worthing</a>, <a href="/wiki/West_Sussex" title="West Sussex">West Sussex</a>, England.</li>
<li>Romero Center Ministries in <a href="/wiki/Camden,_New_Jersey" title="Camden, New Jersey">Camden, New Jersey</a>, U.S., provides Catholic education and retreat experiences inspired by Archbishop Óscar Romero's prophetic witness.  The mission of Romero Center Ministries is to "seek personal, communal, and societal transformation by living ministry as proclaimed in Christ's Gospel."  The center hosts over 1,600 guests annually from high schools, colleges, and youth groups which participate in the Urban Challenge program.<sup id="cite_ref-132" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-132">&#91;132&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><a href="/wiki/The_University_of_Scranton" class="mw-redirect" title="The University of Scranton">The University of Scranton</a> in <a href="/wiki/Scranton,_Pennsylvania" title="Scranton, Pennsylvania">Scranton, Pennsylvania</a>, renamed a plaza of four residence halls after him in 2018, the group of buildings in now known as Romero Plaza.<sup id="cite_ref-133" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-133">&#91;133&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The <a href="/wiki/University_of_Texas_at_El_Paso" title="University of Texas at El Paso">University of Texas at El Paso</a>’s Catholic <a href="/wiki/Newman_Center" title="Newman Center">Newman Center</a> named it’s Ministry Center after the Bishop in 2019<sup id="cite_ref-134" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-134">&#91;134&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Television_and_film">Television and film</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=%C3%93scar_Romero&amp;action=edit&amp;section=26" title="Edit section: Television and film">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>The opening scene in the otherwise fictional spy film <a href="/wiki/S.A.S._%C3%A0_San_Salvador" title="S.A.S. à San Salvador">S.A.S. à San Salvador</a> (1983) shows a car carrying thugs through <a href="/wiki/San_Salvador" title="San Salvador">San Salvador</a> and stopping at a church inside which the main villain assassinates Óscar Romero.</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Oliver_Stone" title="Oliver Stone">Oliver Stone</a>'s 1986 film <i><a href="/wiki/Salvador_(film)" title="Salvador (film)">Salvador</a></i> depicts a fictionalized version of the assassination of Romero (played by <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Carlos_Ruiz" title="José Carlos Ruiz">José Carlos Ruiz</a>) in a pivotal scene.<sup id="cite_ref-135" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-135">&#91;135&#93;</a></sup>  Romero's assassination (with <a href="/wiki/Ren%C3%A9_Enr%C3%ADquez" title="René Enríquez">René Enríquez</a> as Romero) was also featured in the 1983 <a href="/wiki/Television_film" title="Television film">television film</a> <i><a href="/wiki/Choices_of_the_Heart" title="Choices of the Heart">Choices of the Heart</a></i> about the life and death of American Catholic missionary <a href="/wiki/Jean_Donovan" title="Jean Donovan">Jean Donovan</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-136" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-136">&#91;136&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>The Archbishop's life is the basis of the 1989 film <i><a href="/wiki/Romero_(film)" title="Romero (film)">Romero</a></i>, directed by <a href="/wiki/John_Duigan" title="John Duigan">John Duigan</a> and starring <a href="/wiki/Raul_Julia" title="Raul Julia">Raul Julia</a> as Romero. It was produced by Paulist Productions (a film company run by the <a href="/wiki/Paulist_Fathers" title="Paulist Fathers">Paulist Fathers</a>, a Roman Catholic society of priests). Timed for release ten years after Romero's death, it was the first Hollywood feature film ever to be financed by the order. The film received respectful, if less-than-enthusiastic, reviews. <a href="/wiki/Roger_Ebert" title="Roger Ebert">Roger Ebert</a> typified the critics who acknowledged that "The film has a good heart, and the Julia performance is an interesting one, restrained and considered. ...The film's weakness is a certain implacable predictability."<sup id="cite_ref-137" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-137">&#91;137&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>In 2005, while at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_California,_Berkeley_Graduate_School_of_Journalism" class="mw-redirect" title="University of California, Berkeley Graduate School of Journalism">UC Berkeley Graduate School of Journalism</a>, Daniel Freed,<sup id="cite_ref-138" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-138">&#91;138&#93;</a></sup> an independent documentary filmmaker and frequent contributor to <a href="/wiki/PBS" title="PBS">PBS</a> and <a href="/wiki/CNBC" title="CNBC">CNBC</a>, made a 30-minute film entitled <i>The Murder of Monseñor</i><sup id="cite_ref-139" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-139">&#91;139&#93;</a></sup> which not only documented Romero's assassination but also told the story of how Álvaro Rafael Saravia – whom a US District court found, in 2004, had personally organized the assassination – moved to the United States and lived for 25 years as a used car salesman in <a href="/wiki/Modesto,_California" title="Modesto, California">Modesto, California</a>, until he became aware of the pending legal action against him in 2003 and disappeared, leaving behind his drivers license and social security card, as well as his credit cards and his dog.  In 2016 a 1993 law protecting the actions of the military during the Civil War was overruled by a Salvadoran high court and on 23 October 2018, another court ordered the arrest of Saravia.<sup id="cite_ref-thetabletwarrant_85-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-thetabletwarrant-85">&#91;85&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/The_Daily_Show" title="The Daily Show">The Daily Show</a></i> episode on 17 March 2010 showed clips from the Texas State Board of Education in which "a panel of experts" recommended including Romero in the state's history books,<sup id="cite_ref-140" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-140">&#91;140&#93;</a></sup> but an amendment proposed by Patricia Hardy<sup id="cite_ref-141" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-141">&#91;141&#93;</a></sup> to exclude Romero was passed on 10 March 2010. The clip of Ms. Hardy shows her arguing against including Romero because "I guarantee you most of you did not know who Oscar Romero was. ...I just happen to think it's not [important]."<sup id="cite_ref-142" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-142">&#91;142&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>A film about the Archbishop, <i>Monseñor, the Last Journey of Óscar Romero</i>, with the priest Robert Pelton serving as executive producer, had its United States premiere in 2010. This film won the Latin American Studies Association (LASA) Award for Merit in film, in competition with 25 other films. Pelton was invited to show the film throughout Cuba.  It was sponsored by ecclesial and human rights groups from Latin America and from North America.<sup id="cite_ref-143" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-143">&#91;143&#93;</a></sup> <a href="/wiki/Alma_Guillermoprieto" title="Alma Guillermoprieto">Alma Guillermoprieto</a> in <i><a href="/wiki/The_New_York_Review_of_Books" title="The New York Review of Books">The New York Review of Books</a></i> describes the film as a "hagiography," and as "an astonishing compilation of footage" of the final three years of his life.<sup id="cite_ref-144" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-144">&#91;144&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Visual_arts">Visual arts</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=%C3%93scar_Romero&amp;action=edit&amp;section=27" title="Edit section: Visual arts">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/St._James_the_Greater" class="mw-redirect" title="St. James the Greater">St. James the Greater</a> Catholic Church in <a href="/wiki/Charles_Town,_West_Virginia" title="Charles Town, West Virginia">Charles Town, West Virginia</a> is the first known Catholic Church in the United States to venerate St. Oscar Romero with a <a href="/wiki/Stained_glass" title="Stained glass">stained glass</a> window in its building. The project was led by the first Spanish priest of the Wheeling-Charleston Diocese, José Escalante, who is originally from El Salvador, as a gift to the Spanish community of the parish.</li>
<li>John Roberts sculpted a statue of Óscar Romero that fills a prominent niche on the western facade of Westminster Abbey in London; it was unveiled in the presence of <a href="/wiki/Elizabeth_II_of_the_United_Kingdom" class="mw-redirect" title="Elizabeth II of the United Kingdom">Queen Elizabeth II</a> in 1998.<sup id="cite_ref-abbey_145-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-abbey-145">&#91;145&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>Joan Walsh-Smith sculpted a statue of Saint Óscar Romero at The Holy Cross College Ellenbrook Western Australia in 2017. The sculpture depicts their College Patron "walking his faith" on his journey with the poor in El Salvador.<sup id="cite_ref-146" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-146">&#91;146&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>
<ul class="gallery mw-gallery-traditional">
		<li class="gallerybox" style="width: 235px">

<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Music">Music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=%C3%93scar_Romero&amp;action=edit&amp;section=28" title="Edit section: Music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>Violinist <a href="/wiki/Jean-Luc_Ponty" title="Jean-Luc Ponty">Jean-Luc Ponty</a>'s album <i><a href="/wiki/Individual_Choice" title="Individual Choice">Individual Choice</a></i> has a song dedicated to Oscar Romero: "Eulogy to Oscar Romero".</li>
<li>Singer <a href="/wiki/Billy_Bragg" title="Billy Bragg">Billy Bragg</a>, on "The Marching Song Of The Covert Battalions", from his 1990 EP, <a href="/wiki/The_Internationale_(album)" title="The Internationale (album)">The Internationale</a>, shouts Oscar Romero's surname after the line, "Away with nuns and bishops".</li>
<li>Panamanian musician Rubén Blades dedicated a song to him named <a href="https://es.wikipedia.org/wiki/El" class="extiw" title="es:El">Padre Antonio y el Monaguillo Andrés|El Padre Antonio y el Monaguillo Andrés</a></li></ul>
<p><sup id="cite_ref-147" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-147">&#91;147&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Political_writing">Political writing</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=%C3%93scar_Romero&amp;action=edit&amp;section=29" title="Edit section: Political writing">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li>In their book <i><a href="/wiki/Manufacturing_Consent" title="Manufacturing Consent">Manufacturing Consent</a></i> (1988), <a href="/wiki/Noam_Chomsky" title="Noam Chomsky">Noam Chomsky</a> and <a href="/wiki/Edward_S._Herman" title="Edward S. Herman">Edward S. Herman</a> compared US media coverage of the murders of Romero and other Latin American clergy in US client states with coverage of the murder of Catholic priest <a href="/wiki/Jerzy_Popie%C5%82uszko" title="Jerzy Popiełuszko">Jerzy Popiełuszko</a> in "enemy" Communist <a href="/wiki/Poland" title="Poland">Poland</a> to explain their <a href="/wiki/Propaganda_model" title="Propaganda model">propaganda model</a> hypothesis.<sup id="cite_ref-148" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-148">&#91;148&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-Goodwin_149-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Goodwin-149">&#91;149&#93;</a></sup></li></ul>

<li id="cite_note-86"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-86">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1133582631"><cite id="CITEREFPaul_D._Newpower,_M.M.Stephen_T._DeMott,_M.M.1983" class="citation web cs1">Paul D. United States. Archived from <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="http://www.americancatholic.org/Features/JohnPaulII/3-CentralAmerica-1983.asp">the original</a> on 2 February 2013<span class="reference-accessdate">. Retrieved <span class="nowrap">1 January</span> 2013</span>. <q>The pontiff went on to proclaim Archbishop Romero as "a zealous and venerated pastor who tried to stop violence. I ask that his memory be always respected, and let no ideological interest try to distort his sacrifice as a pastor given over to his flock." The right-wing groups did not want to hear that. They portray Romero as one who stirred the poor to violence. The other papal gesture that drew diverse reactions in El Salvador and rankled the Reagan administration was the pope's use of the word dialogue in talking about steps toward ending the civil war. A month before John Paul II journeyed to Central America, U.S. government representatives visited the Vatican and El Salvador to persuade church officials to have the pope mention elections rather than dialogue.</q></cite><span title="ctx_ver=Z39.88-2004&amp;rft_val_fmt=info%3Aofi%2Ffmt%3Akev%3Amtx%3Ajournal&amp;rft.genre=unknown&amp;rft.jtitle=St.+Anthony+Messenger&amp;rft.atitle=Pope+John+Paul+II+in+Central+America%3A+What+Did+His+Trip+Accomplish%3F&amp;rft.date=1983-06&amp;rft.au=Paul+D.+Newpower%2C+M.M.&amp;rft.au=Stephen+T.+DeMott%2C+M.M.&amp;rft_id=http%3A%2F%2Fwww.americancatholic.org%2FFeatures%2FJohnPaulII%2F3-CentralAmerica-1983.asp&amp;rfr_id=info%3Asid%2Fen.wikipedia.org%3A%C3%93scar+Romero" class="Z3988"></span></span>
